To see this, consider the map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _* :M_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n} \times B_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}&\rightarrow B_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n} \\ \left( \prod _{i=1}^n X_i^{q_i}, \otimes _{i=1}^n \textsf{e}_{j_i} \right)&\mapsto \otimes _{i=1}^n \textsf{e}_{j_i + q_i \bmod m_i}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ∗:Mm1,⋯,mn×Bm1,⋯,mn→Bm1,⋯,mn∏i=1nXii,⊗i=1neji↦⊗i=1neji+qimodmi,for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le j_i \le m_i - 1$$\end{document}0≤ji≤mi-1, which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_2$$\end{document}F2-linearly extends to the map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu :R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n} \times V_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n} \rightarrow V_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ:Rm1,⋯,mn×Vm1,⋯,mn→Vm1,⋯,mn.Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Vm1,⋯,mn is an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn-module under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ.

Definition 8

The natural \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn-action on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}^{\omega }$$\end{document}Vm1,⋯,mnω for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in \mathbb {Z}_{>0}$$\end{document}ω∈Z>0 induced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ is called the circulant module of rank \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω.

Proposition 11

A circulant module of rank \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω is a free \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn-module of rank \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω.

Proof

It suffices to show this for circulant modules of rank 1. Consider the following natural 1-to-1 mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta :B_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n} \rightarrow M_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}ϑ:Bm1,⋯,mn→Mm1,⋯,mn such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vartheta _* :B_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n} \rightarrow M_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}, \ \otimes _{i=1}^n \textsf{e}_{j_i} \mapsto \prod _{i=1}^n X_i^{j_i}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϑ∗:Bm1,⋯,mn→Mm1,⋯,mn,⊗i=1neji↦∏i=1nXii,which linearly extends to the bijective map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vartheta :V_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n} \rightarrow R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϑ:Vm1,⋯,mn→Rm1,⋯,mn.This map can be easily verified to be a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn-linear map, hence we have constructed a natural \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn-isomorphism. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 3

From the above proposition, we have the commutative diagram

where the dot in the lower row is the natural product operation of the ring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn. The vertical maps are one-to-one correspondences, which implies that the circulant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn-module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Vm1,⋯,mn is indeed free of rank one, with corresponding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn-module isomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta $$\end{document}ϑ.

Example 2

We show an example of a circulant module in action. Consider the vector space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{16, 64}:= \mathbb {F}_2^{16} \otimes _{\mathbb {F}_2} \mathbb {F}_2^{64}$$\end{document}V16,64:=F216⊗F2F264, and the circulant ring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{16,64}$$\end{document}R16,64. Consider the vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v = \textsf{e}_{12} \otimes _{\mathbb {F}_2} \textsf{e}_{55} \in V_{16,64}$$\end{document}v=e12⊗F2e55∈V16,64, and the polynomial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:= X^{14} Y^{21} + X^3 Y^9 + 1 \in R_{16,64}$$\end{document}f:=X14Y21+X3Y9+1∈R16,64. Observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta (v) = X^{12} Y^{55}$$\end{document}ϑ(v)=X12Y55, and so\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f \cdot \vartheta (v)&= (X^{14} Y^{21} + X^3 Y^9 + 1) \cdot X^{12} Y^{55} = X^{26} Y^{76} + X^{15} Y^{64} + X^{12} Y^{55} \\&\equiv X^{10} Y^{12} + X^{15} + X^{12} Y^{55}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f·ϑ(v)=(X14Y21+X3Y9+1)·X12Y55=X26Y76+X15Y64+X12Y55≡X10Y12+X15+X12Y55,in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{16,64}$$\end{document}R16,64. From the commutative diagram in Remark 3, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu (f,v)&:= \vartheta ^{-1}(f \cdot \vartheta (v)) = \vartheta ^{-1}(X^{10} Y^{12} + X^{15} + X^{12} Y^{55}) \\&= \textsf{e}_{10} \otimes _{\mathbb {F}_2} \textsf{e}_{12} + \textsf{e}_{15} \otimes _{\mathbb {F}_2} \textsf{e}_0 + \textsf{e}_{12} \otimes _{\mathbb {F}_2} \textsf{e}_{55}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ(f,v):=ϑ-1(f·ϑ(v))=ϑ-1(X10Y12+X15+X12Y55)=e10⊗F2e12+e15⊗F2e0+e12⊗F2e55.

Example 3

Consider the ring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_m = \mathbb {F}_2 [X] / \langle X^m - 1 \rangle $$\end{document}Rm=F2[X]/⟨Xm-1⟩. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{e}_i \in \mathbb {F}_2^m$$\end{document}ei∈F2m and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^j \in R_m$$\end{document}Xj∈Rm, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le i,j < m$$\end{document}0≤i,j<m. By definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu (X^j, \textsf{e}_i) = \textsf{e}_{i + j}$$\end{document}μ(Xj,ei)=ei+j. Note that this is exactly the same as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{circ}(\textsf{e}_j) \cdot \textsf{e}_i$$\end{document}circ(ej)·ei, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{circ}(\textsf{e}_j)$$\end{document}circ(ej) refers to the circulant matrix with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{e}_j$$\end{document}ej as its first column. Since this is true for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le i,j < m$$\end{document}0≤i,j<m, and since the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^j$$\end{document}Xj and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{e}_i$$\end{document}ei span \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_m$$\end{document}Rm and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_2^m$$\end{document}F2m respectively, one can verify that the circulant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_m$$\end{document}Rm-module is indeed equivalent to the module induced on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_2^m$$\end{document}F2m over the ring of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \times m$$\end{document}m×m-circulant matrices over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_2$$\end{document}F2, which was described at the beginning of this section. This justifies the viewpoint that circulant modules can be considered generalizations of the notion of circulant matrices.

Example 4

Consider the ring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{4,32}$$\end{document}R4,32. An interesting case is the free module action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{4,32}$$\end{document}R4,32 over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathbb {F}_2^4 \otimes \mathbb {F}_2^{32})^3$$\end{document}(F24⊗F232)3. By Proposition 11, this module is isomorphic to the free module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{4,32}^3$$\end{document}R4,323, which is in particular useful for studying the linear layer of Xoodoo, which we cover in Sect. 5.

Classification of local circulant rings

We prove that a circulant ring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn is a local ring if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_i$$\end{document}mi is a power of 2 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le i \le m$$\end{document}1≤i≤m.

Lemma 12

The ideal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_{n}$$\end{document}mn is the unique maximal ideal containing the ideal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {a}_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}}$$\end{document}a2l1,⋯,2ln, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_1, \dotsc ,l_n \in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}l1,⋯,ln∈Z≥0.

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_2[X_1, \dotsc ,X_n]$$\end{document}F2[X1,⋯,Xn] has characteristic 2, we have that4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X^{2^{l_i}}-1 = (X-1)^{2^{l_i}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Xli-1=(X-1)li,for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le i \le n$$\end{document}1≤i≤n, which immediately implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {a}_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}} \subseteq \mathfrak {m}_n$$\end{document}a2l1,⋯,2ln⊆mn. Note that (4) also implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_n$$\end{document}mn is contained in the radical of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {a}_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}}$$\end{document}a2l1,⋯,2ln, which in turn implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_n = r(\mathfrak {a}_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}})$$\end{document}mn=r(a2l1,⋯,2ln). Since every maximal ideal containing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {a}_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}}$$\end{document}a2l1,⋯,2ln must contain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(\mathfrak {a}_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}})$$\end{document}r(a2l1,⋯,2ln), it must also contain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_n$$\end{document}mn. But \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_n$$\end{document}mn is already maximal, hence uniqueness is proven. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Theorem 13

A circulant ring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn is a local ring if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_i$$\end{document}mi is a power of 2 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le i \le m$$\end{document}1≤i≤m.

Proof

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Leftarrow )$$\end{document}(⇐) - Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_i$$\end{document}mi is of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{l_i}$$\end{document}2li, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_i \in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}li∈Z≥0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le i \le n$$\end{document}1≤i≤n. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_n$$\end{document}mn is the unique maximal ideal containing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {a}_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}}$$\end{document}a2l1,⋯,2ln by the above lemma, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathfrak {m}}_n$$\end{document}m¯n must be the unique maximal ideal in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}}$$\end{document}R2l1,⋯,2ln as shown in [6]. This shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}}$$\end{document}R2l1,⋯,2ln is local.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Rightarrow )$$\end{document}(⇒) - Assume that there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_j$$\end{document}mj for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le j \le n$$\end{document}1≤j≤n such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_j$$\end{document}mj is not a power of 2. We may assume without loss of generality that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_1$$\end{document}m1 is not a power of 2. Consider the ideal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}':= \langle \Phi _{m_1} (X_1), X_2 - 1, \dotsc , X_n - 1 \rangle $$\end{document}m′:=⟨Φm1(X1),X2-1,⋯,Xn-1⟩ where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{m_1}$$\end{document}Φm1 is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_1$$\end{document}m1-th cyclotomic polynomial. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{m_1}$$\end{document}Φm1 has degree larger than 1, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_1$$\end{document}m1 is not a power of 2. By the third isomorphism theorem for rings, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n} / \overline{\mathfrak {m}}'&= (\mathbb {F}_2[X_1, \dotsc ,X_n] / \mathfrak {a}_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n} ) / (\mathfrak {m}' / \mathfrak {a}_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n} ) \\&\cong \mathbb {F}_2[X_1, \dotsc ,X_n] / \mathfrak {m}' \cong \mathbb {F}_2[X_1] / \Phi _{m_1}(X_1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn/m¯′=(F2[X1,⋯,Xn]/am1,⋯,mn)/(m′/am1,⋯,mn)≅F2[X1,⋯,Xn]/m′≅F2[X1]/Φm1(X1).Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{m_1}$$\end{document}Φm1 is irreducible in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_2[X]$$\end{document}F2[X], we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_2[X_1] / \Phi _{m_1}(X_1)$$\end{document}F2[X1]/Φm1(X1) is a field isomorphic to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {GF}}(2^{\deg (\Phi _{m_1})})$$\end{document}GF(2deg(Φm1)). Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathfrak {m}}'$$\end{document}m¯′ is a maximal ideal of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn which is not equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_n$$\end{document}mn, thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{m_1, \dotsc ,m_n}$$\end{document}Rm1,⋯,mn is not a local ring. This concludes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Note 14

For the remainder of this section, we denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathfrak {m}}_n$$\end{document}m¯n simply by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_n$$\end{document}mn, which will not cause confusion due to uniqueness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_n$$\end{document}mn.

Lemma 15

The residue field of a local circulant ring R is isomorphic to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_2$$\end{document}F2, with quotient map\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q_R :R \rightarrow \mathbb {F}_2, \ f \mapsto f(1^n). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}qR:R→F2,f↦f(1n).

Proof

By the third isomorphism theorem for rings, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}} / \overline{\mathfrak {m}}_n&= (\mathbb {F}_2[X_1, \dotsc ,X_n] / \mathfrak {a}_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}}) / (\mathfrak {m}_n / \mathfrak {a}_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}}) \\&\cong \mathbb {F}_2[X_1, \dotsc ,X_n] / \mathfrak {m}_n \cong \mathbb {F}_2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R2l1,⋯,2ln/m¯n=(F2[X1,⋯,Xn]/a2l1,⋯,2ln)/(mn/a2l1,⋯,2ln)≅F2[X1,⋯,Xn]/mn≅F2,where by construction the last isomorphism is indeed the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \mapsto f(1^n)$$\end{document}f↦f(1n). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 4

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in R$$\end{document}f∈R, we denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_R(f)$$\end{document}qR(f) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{f}$$\end{document}f¯.

Corollary 16

For R a local circulant ring, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in R$$\end{document}f∈R is invertible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(1^n) \ne 0$$\end{document}f(1n)≠0.

Proof

Since R is a local ring, we have that f is invertible if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \notin \mathfrak {m}_n$$\end{document}f∉mn. This is indeed equivalent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(1^n) \ne 0$$\end{document}f(1n)≠0 by the above lemma. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Corollary 17

For R a local circulant ring, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in R$$\end{document}f∈R is invertible if and only if f contains an odd number of terms.

Proof

By the above corollary, we conclude that every term is invertible. If f has t terms, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(1^n) \equiv t \bmod 2$$\end{document}f(1n)≡tmod2 which is not equal to 0 if and only if t is odd. This concludes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

General linear group over local circulant rings

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R:= R_{2^{l_1}, \dotsc ,2^{l_n}}$$\end{document}R:=R2l1,⋯,2ln be a local circulant ring, and consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_R$$\end{document}qR as in Lemma 15. This map can be extended to the map of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \times m$$\end{document}m×m-matrices\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q_{m,R} :{\text {M}}_m(R) \rightarrow {\text {M}}_m(\mathbb {F}_2), \ A := (A_{ij})_{0 \le i,j \le m-1} \mapsto (\overline{A_{ij}})_{0 \le i,j \le m-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}qm,R:Mm(R)→Mm(F2),A:=(Aij)0≤i,j≤m-1↦(Aij¯)0≤i,j≤m-1.

Remark 5

Just as for the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_R$$\end{document}qR, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \in {\text {M}}_m(\mathbb {F}_2)$$\end{document}A∈Mm(F2), we denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{m,R}(A)$$\end{document}qm,R(A) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{A}$$\end{document}A¯.

Observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det (\overline{A}) = \overline{\det (A)}$$\end{document}det(A¯)=det(A)¯, since the expression of the determinant consists of finite sums of finite products of entries of A, which split under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_R$$\end{document}qR. This implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{m,R}$$\end{document}qm,R maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {GL}}_m(R)$$\end{document}GLm(R) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {GL}}_m(\mathbb {F}_2)$$\end{document}GLm(F2). Moreover, we have that the preimage of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {GL}}_m(\mathbb {F}_2)$$\end{document}GLm(F2) under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{m,R}$$\end{document}qm,R is exactly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {GL}}_m(R)$$\end{document}GLm(R), as a result of the following lemma:

Lemma 18

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \in {\text {M}}_m(R)$$\end{document}A∈Mm(R). Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \in {\text {GL}}_m(R)$$\end{document}A∈GLm(R) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{A} \in {\text {GL}}_m(\mathbb {F}_2)$$\end{document}A¯∈GLm(F2).

Proof

Due to locality of R, we have the following equivalent statements:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A \in {\text {GL}}_m(R)&\Leftrightarrow \det (A) \in R^* \Leftrightarrow \det (A) \notin \mathfrak {m}_n \Leftrightarrow \det (\overline{A}) \in \mathbb {F}_2^* \Leftrightarrow \overline{A} \in {\text {GL}}_m(\mathbb {F}_2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A∈GLm(R)⇔det(A)∈R∗⇔det(A)∉mn⇔det(A¯)∈F2∗⇔A¯∈GLm(F2).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The above lemma implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{m,R} \mid _{{\text {GL}}_m(R)} :{\text {GL}}_m(R) \rightarrow {\text {GL}}_m(\mathbb {F}_2)$$\end{document}qm,R∣GLm(R):GLm(R)→GLm(F2) is a surjective group homomorphism. Let us denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{m,R} \mid _{{\text {GL}}_m(R)}$$\end{document}qm,R∣GLm(R) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_{m,R}^*$$\end{document}qm,R∗. From Lemma 18, we conclude that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ker (q_{m,R}^*) = \{ I_m + A : A \in {\text {M}}_m(\mathfrak {m}_n) \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ker(qm,R∗)={Im+A:A∈Mm(mn)}.This implies that5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \# \ker (q_{m,R}^*) = \# {\text {M}}_m(\mathfrak {m}_n) = (\# \mathfrak {m}_n)^{m^2} = \left( 2^{( \prod _{i=1}^n 2^{l_i} ) - 1} \right) ^{m^2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}#ker(qm,R∗)=#Mm(mn)=(#mn)2=2(∏i=1n2li)-12,which in particular means that the order of the group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ker (q_{m,R}^*)$$\end{document}ker(qm,R∗) is a power of 2. By Lagrange’s theorem, the order of an element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_m + A \in \ker (q_{m,R}^*)$$\end{document}Im+A∈ker(qm,R∗), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \in {\text {M}}_m(\mathfrak {m}_n)$$\end{document}A∈Mm(mn), is of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{\lambda }$$\end{document}2λ where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}λ∈Z≥0. Note that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (I_m +A)^{2^{\lambda }} = I_m + A^{2^{\lambda }}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Im+A)λ=Im+Aλ,by the binomial theorem of Newton, and since R is of characteristic 2. Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {ord}}(I_m + A)$$\end{document}ord(Im+A) is the smallest number of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{\lambda }$$\end{document}2λ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{2^{\lambda }} = 0_{m \times m}$$\end{document}Aλ=0m×m. In particular, A is a nilpotent matrix.

Lemma 19

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \in {\text {M}}_m(\mathfrak {m}_n)$$\end{document}A∈Mm(mn) and define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l = \max (l_i: 1 \le i \le n)$$\end{document}l=max(li:1≤i≤n). Then we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{n \cdot 2^l} = 0_{m \times m}$$\end{document}An·2l=0m×m.

Proof

In this proof, we use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0}^n$$\end{document}Z≥0n as an index set, and we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{e}_i$$\end{document}ei be the i-th unit vector in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0}^n$$\end{document}Z≥0n where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \le i \le n-1$$\end{document}0≤i≤n-1.

By assumption of the lemma, there exist matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{\textsf{e}_i} \in {\text {M}}_m(R)$$\end{document}Aei∈Mm(R) such that6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A = \sum _{i=1}^n (X_i - 1) \cdot A_{\textsf{e}_i}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A=∑i=1n(Xi-1)·Aei.From this, we can construct matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{j_1 \textsf{e}_1 + \cdots + j_n \textsf{e}_n} \in {\text {M}}_m(R)$$\end{document}Aj1e1+⋯+jnen∈Mm(R) such that7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A^2 = \sum _{\begin{array}{c} 0 \le j_1, \dotsc ,j_n \le 2 \\ j_1 + \cdots + j_n = 2 \end{array}} A_{j_1 \textsf{e}_1 + \cdots + j_n \textsf{e}_n} \cdot \prod _{i=1}^n (X_i - 1)^{j_i}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A2=∑0≤j1,⋯,jn≤2j1+⋯+jn=2Aj1e1+⋯+jnen·∏i=1n(Xi-1)ji,where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A_{j_1 \textsf{e}_1 + \cdots + j_n \textsf{e}_n} = \sum _{\begin{array}{c} 0 \le j_1, \dotsc ,j_n \le 2 \\ j_1 + \cdots + j_n = 2 \end{array}} A_{\textsf{e}_i}^{j_i}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Aj1e1+⋯+jnen=∑0≤j1,⋯,jn≤2j1+⋯+jn=2Aeii.Note that the matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{\textsf{e}_i}$$\end{document}Aei satisfying (6) are not unique. For the proof, it suffices to only knowing its existence.

By inductively applying this reasoning, one can show that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \mathbb {Z}_{>0}$$\end{document}k∈Z>0, there exists a family of matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{j_1 \textsf{e}_1 + \cdots + j_n \textsf{e}_n} \in {\text {M}}_m(R)$$\end{document}Aj1e1+⋯+jnen∈Mm(R) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j_1 + \cdots + j_n = k$$\end{document}j1+⋯+jn=k such that8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A^k = \sum _{\begin{array}{c} 0 \le j_1, \dotsc ,j_n \le k \\ j_1 + \cdots + j_n = k \end{array}} A_{j_1 \textsf{e}_1 + \cdots + j_n \textsf{e}_n} \cdot \prod _{i=1}^n (X_i - 1)^{j_i}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ak=∑0≤j1,⋯,jn≤kj1+⋯+jn=kAj1e1+⋯+jnen·∏i=1n(Xi-1)ji.When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \ge n \cdot 2^l$$\end{document}k≥n·2l, we must have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j_i \ge 2^{l_i}$$\end{document}ji≥2li for some i, which implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\prod _{i=1}^n (X_i - 1)^{j_i} \in \mathfrak {m}_n$$\end{document}∏i=1n(Xi-1)ji∈mn for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j_1, \dotsc ,j_n$$\end{document}j1,⋯,jn satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j_1 + \cdots + j_n = k$$\end{document}j1+⋯+jn=k. Hence we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A^{n \cdot 2^l} = 0_{m \times m} \in {\text {M}}_m(R)$$\end{document}An·2l=0m×m∈Mm(R) by applying Eq. (8), which concludes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Corollary 20

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \in \ker (q_{m,R}^*)$$\end{document}B∈ker(qm,R∗), we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {ord}}(B) \mid 2^{l + \lceil \log _2(n) \rceil }$$\end{document}ord(B)∣2l+⌈log2(n)⌉.

Proof

Every element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \in \ker (q_{m,R}^*)$$\end{document}B∈ker(qm,R∗) is of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_m + C$$\end{document}Im+C, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \in {\text {M}}_m(\mathfrak {m}_n)$$\end{document}C∈Mm(mn). Observe that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 2^{l + \lceil \log _2(n) \rceil } = 2^{\lceil \log _2(n) \rceil } \cdot 2^l \ge n \cdot 2^l. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}2l+⌈log2(n)⌉=2⌈log2(n)⌉·2l≥n·2l.From this identity together with Lemma 19, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} B^{2^{l + \lceil \log _2(n) \rceil }}&= (I_m + C)^{2^{l + \lceil \log _2(n) \rceil }} = I_m^{2^{l + \lceil \log _2(n) \rceil }} + C^{2^{l + \lceil \log _2(n) \rceil }} = I_m + 0_{m \times m} \\&= I_m, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Bl+⌈log2(n)⌉=(Im+C)l+⌈log2(n)⌉=Iml+⌈log2(n)⌉+Cl+⌈log2(n)⌉=Im+0m×m=Im,which concludes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Column parity mixers

Using circulant modules covered in Sect. 3, we introduce a new approach to column parity mixers (CPMs) which is a vast generalization of circulant column parity mixers (CCPMs) defined in [9].

Definition 9

Let R be a commutative ring with unity. A column parity mixer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _z$$\end{document}θz (or CPM for short) over R of dimension m where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z = (z_0, \dotsc ,z_{m-1})^{\textsf{T}} \in R^m$$\end{document}z=(z0,⋯,zm-1)T∈Rm, is an R-endomorphism over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^m$$\end{document}Rm represented by the matrix\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _z = I_m + {\text {col}}(z) = \begin{pmatrix} 1_R + z_0 & z_0 & z_0 & \cdots & z_0 \\ z_1 & 1_R + z_1 & z_1 & \cdots & z_1 \\ z_2 & z_2 & 1_R + z_2 & \cdots & z_2 \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ z_{m-1} & z_{m-1} & z_{m-1} & \cdots & 1_R + z_{m-1} \end{pmatrix} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θz=Im+col(z)=1R+z0z0z0⋯z0z11R+z1z1⋯z1z2z21R+z2⋯z2⋮⋮⋮⋱⋮zm-1zm-1zm-1⋯1R+zm-1.We say that z is the parity-folding matrix array, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0, \dotsc ,z_{m-1}$$\end{document}z0,⋯,zm-1 are the parity-folding matrices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _z$$\end{document}θz. The set of all CPMs over R of dimension m is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {CPM}}_m(R)$$\end{document}CPMm(R).

A CPM over a circulant ring R is called a circulant column parity mixer, or CCPM for short.

Remark 6

Although the terminology might suggest otherwise, parity folding matrices are not necessarily matrices, but rather elements in the ring R. The reason for this choice of terminology is that the analogue of parity-folding matrices in the original definition of CPMs in [9] are actually matrices. These two notions coincide in the case when R is the ring of circulant matrices.

Remark 7

The CPMs in Definition 9 are closely related to, but not a full generalization of CPMs introduced in [9], which we refer to for now as the original CPMs. This is because the parity folding matrices in the original CPMs admit all matrices in the matrix ring, which do not form a commutative ring.

Definition 9 is however a generalization of the original CCPMs. In the special case where we consider CCPMs of Definition 9, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0 = z_1 = \cdots = z_{m-1}$$\end{document}z0=z1=⋯=zm-1, we obtain the original CCPMs, where they use only one parity folding matrix.

Characteristic polynomial and determinant

We give an expression of the characteristic polynomial and the determinant of a CPM in terms of its parity-folding matrices. We assume for the remainder of this subsection that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _z \in {\text {CPM}}_m(R)$$\end{document}θz∈CPMm(R) for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \in \mathbb {Z}_{>0}$$\end{document}m∈Z>0 and commutative ring R.

Theorem 21

The characteristic polynomial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\theta _z}(\lambda )$$\end{document}pθz(λ) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _z$$\end{document}θz is9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_{\theta _z}(\lambda ) = \left( \left( 1_R + \sum _{i=0}^{m-1} z_i \right) - \lambda \right) \cdot (1_R - \lambda )^{m-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pθz(λ)=1R+∑i=0m-1zi-λ·(1R-λ)m-1.

Proof

By definition, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\theta _z}(\lambda ):= \det (\theta _z - \lambda \cdot I_m)$$\end{document}pθz(λ):=det(θz-λ·Im). To compute the determinant of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _z - \lambda \cdot I_m$$\end{document}θz-λ·Im, we use the property that adding up rows (or columns) to other rows (or columns) will not affect the determinant. By adding the first column vector to all the other column vectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _z$$\end{document}θz, followed by adding up all the row vectors from the second till the last row vector to the first row vector, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det (\theta _z - \lambda \cdot I_m)&= \begin{vmatrix} 1_R + z_0 - \lambda&z_0&z_0&\cdots&z_0 \\ z_1&1_R + z_1 - \lambda&z_1&\cdots&z_1 \\ z_2&z_2&1_R + z_2 - \lambda&\cdots&z_2 \\ \vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots \\ z_{m-1}&z_{m-1}&z_{m-1}&\cdots&1_R + z_{m-1} - \lambda \end{vmatrix} \\&= \begin{vmatrix} 1_R + z_0 - \lambda&\lambda - 1_R&\lambda - 1_R&\cdots&\lambda - 1_R \\ z_1&1_R - \lambda&0&\cdots&0 \\ z_2&0&1_R - \lambda&\cdots&0 \\ \vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots \\ z_{m-1}&0&0&\cdots&1_R - \lambda \end{vmatrix} \\&= \begin{vmatrix} 1_R + \left( \sum _{i=0}^{m-1}z_i \right) - \lambda&0&0&\cdots&0 \\ z_1&1_R - \lambda&0&\cdots&0 \\ z_2&0&1_R - \lambda&\cdots&0 \\ \vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots \\ z_{m-1}&0&0&\cdots&1_R - \lambda \end{vmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}det(θz-λ·Im)=1R+z0-λz0z0⋯z0z11R+z1-λz1⋯z1z2z21R+z2-λ⋯z2⋮⋮⋮⋱⋮zm-1zm-1zm-1⋯1R+zm-1-λ=1R+z0-λλ-1Rλ-1R⋯λ-1Rz11R-λ0⋯0z201R-λ⋯0⋮⋮⋮⋱⋮zm-100⋯1R-λ=1R+∑i=0m-1zi-λ00⋯0z11R-λ0⋯0z201R-λ⋯0⋮⋮⋮⋱⋮zm-100⋯1R-λ.Denote the last matrix by A and denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{(i,j)}$$\end{document}A(i,j) as the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m-1 \times m-1$$\end{document}m-1×m-1-matrix by removing the i-th row and the j-th column of A. Then10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det (\theta _z - \lambda \cdot I_m)&= \det (A) \nonumber \\&= \sum _{j=0}^{m-1} (-1)^{j} \cdot A_{0,j} \cdot \det (A_{(0,j)}) \nonumber \\&= A_{0,0} \cdot \det (A_{(0.0)}) + \sum _{j=1}^{m-1} (-1)^{j} \cdot A_{0,j} \cdot \det (A_{(0,j)}) \nonumber \\&= A_{0,0} \cdot \det (A_{(0.0)}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}det(θz-λ·Im)=det(A)=∑j=0m-1(-1)j·A0,j·det(A(0,j))=A0,0·det(A(0.0))+∑j=1m-1(-1)j·A0,j·det(A(0,j))=A0,0·det(A(0.0)),where the last equation holds because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{0,j} = 0$$\end{document}A0,j=0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j > 0$$\end{document}j>0. Observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{0,0} = \left( 1_R + \sum _{i=0}^{m-1} z_i \right) - \lambda $$\end{document}A0,0=1R+∑i=0m-1zi-λ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{(0,0)} = (1_R - \lambda ) \cdot I_{m-1}$$\end{document}A(0,0)=(1R-λ)·Im-1, the latter implying that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det (A_{(0,0)}) = (1_R - \lambda )^{m-1}$$\end{document}det(A(0,0))=(1R-λ)m-1. Substituting these values in (10), we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det (\theta _z - \lambda \cdot I_m) = A_{0,0} \cdot \det (A_{(0.0)}) = \left( \left( 1_R + \sum _{i=0}^{m-1} z_i \right) - \lambda \right) \cdot (1_R - \lambda )^{m-1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}det(θz-λ·Im)=A0,0·det(A(0.0))=1R+∑i=0m-1zi-λ·(1R-λ)m-1,which concludes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Corollary 22

The determinant of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _z$$\end{document}θz equals\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det (\theta _z) = 1_R + \sum _{i=0}^{m-1} z_i. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}det(θz)=1R+∑i=0m-1zi.

Proof

The determinant equals the constant term of the characteristic polynomial of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _z$$\end{document}θz, which from Eq. (9) equals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1_R + \sum _{i=0}^{m-1} z_i$$\end{document}1R+∑i=0m-1zi. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Note 23

By the above corollary, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{\theta _z}(\lambda )$$\end{document}pθz(λ) can be expressed as11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_{\theta _z}(\lambda ) = (\det (\theta _z) - \lambda ) \cdot (1_R - \lambda )^{m-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pθz(λ)=(det(θz)-λ)·(1R-λ)m-1.We will use this expression for the remainder of this paper.

Eigenvectors and eigenspaces

Lemma 24

Define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_1 := \left\{ v=(v_0, \dotsc ,v_{m-1})^{\textsf{T}} : \sum _{i=0}^{m-1} v_i = 0 \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E1:=v=(v0,⋯,vm-1)T:∑i=0m-1vi=0.Then all elements in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1$$\end{document}E1 have eigenvalue \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1_R$$\end{document}1R, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1$$\end{document}E1 is a free R-module of rank \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m-1$$\end{document}m-1.

Proof
