Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f = a_d X^d + a_{d-1} X^{d-1} + \cdots + a_1 X + a_0 \in R_n^*$$\end{document}f=adXd+ad-1Xd-1+⋯+a1X+a0∈Rn∗. Since we work over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_2$$\end{document}F2, we have14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}}&= \big (a_d X^d + a_{d-1} X^{d-1} + \cdots + a_1 X + a_0\big )^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fordn(2)=(adXd+ad-1Xd-1+⋯+a1X+a0)ordn(2)15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&= \big (a_d X^d\big )^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}} + \big (a_{d-1} X^{d-1}\big )^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}} + \cdots + (a_1 X)^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}} + a_0^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}=(adXd)ordn(2)+(ad-1Xd-1)ordn(2)+⋯+(a1X)ordn(2)+a0ordn(2)16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&= a_d \left( X^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}} \right) ^d + a_{d-1} \left( X^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}} \right) ^{d-1} + \cdots + a_1 X^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}} + a_0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}=adXordn(2)d+ad-1Xordn(2)d-1+⋯+a1Xordn(2)+a0By definition, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)} \equiv 1 \bmod n$$\end{document}2ordn(2)≡1modn, which implies that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}} \equiv X \bmod \langle X^n - 1 \rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Xordn(2)≡Xmod⟨Xn-1⟩.Hence we can conclude from Expression (16) that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)}} \equiv f \bmod \langle X^n - 1 \rangle $$\end{document}fordn(2)≡fmod⟨Xn-1⟩, implying that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)} - 1} \equiv 1 \bmod \langle X^n - 1 \rangle $$\end{document}f2ordn(2)-1≡1mod⟨Xn-1⟩ by invertibility of f. Thus the order of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in R_n^*$$\end{document}f∈Rn∗ must divide \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{{\text {ord}}_n(2)} - 1$$\end{document}2ordn(2)-1, which concludes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Let us choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n = 167$$\end{document}n=167. Observe that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {ord}}_{167}(2) = 83$$\end{document}ord167(2)=83, which by the above theorem means that the highest possible order of elements in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{167}^*$$\end{document}R167∗ equals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{83} - 1$$\end{document}283-1. Consider\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta = \begin{pmatrix} 1 + f & f & f \\ f & 1 + f & f \\ f & f & 1 + f \end{pmatrix} , \ \ \ \ \rho _l = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & X & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & X^{11} \end{pmatrix}, \ \ \ \ \rho _r = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & X & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & X^{10} \end{pmatrix}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ=1+ffff1+ffff1+f,ρl=1000X000X11,ρr=1000X000X10,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f = X^6 + X^{15}$$\end{document}f=X6+X15. Here we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det (\rho _l \circ \theta \circ \rho _r) = X^{12} \cdot (X^{15} + X^6 + 1) \cdot X^{11} = X^{38} + X^{29} + X^{23}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}det(ρl∘θ∘ρr)=X12·(X15+X6+1)·X11=X38+X29+X23.By using Sagemath, we verified that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{27} + X^{18} + X^{12}$$\end{document}X27+X18+X12 is invertible (see Appendix C for the code). Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{83} - 1$$\end{document}283-1 is a prime number (it is a Mersenne prime number), it must be the order of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{27} + X^{18} + X^{12}$$\end{document}X27+X18+X12. By Lemma 1, we can conclude that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^{83} - 1$$\end{document}283-1 divides \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {ord}}(\rho _l \circ \theta \circ \rho _r)$$\end{document}ord(ρl∘θ∘ρr).

Remark 11

We managed to compute the exact order of the composition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _l \circ \theta \circ \rho _r$$\end{document}ρl∘θ∘ρr, which equals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( 2^{83} - 1 \right) \cdot \lambda $$\end{document}283-1·λ where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda =&\ 301 \, 541 \, 899 \, 055 \, 510 \, 925 \, 582 \, 216 \, 169 \, 150 \, 861 \, 286 \, 153 \, 081 \, 761 \, 757 \, 331 \, 612 \, 351 \\&867 \, 575 \, 029 \, 327 \, 375 \, 019 \\ \approx&\ 1.33 \cdot 2^{247}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ=301541899055510925582216169150861286153081761757331612351867575029327375019≈1.33·2247.This is significantly higher than 32. We illustrate a sketch on how we obtained \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ, which requires a bit of mathematical reasoning.

Computing Strategy (Sketch)

The ring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{167}$$\end{document}R167 can be naturally embedded in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {GF}}\left( 2^{83} \right) [X] / \langle X^{167} - 1 \rangle $$\end{document}GF283[X]/⟨X167-1⟩. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{167} - 1$$\end{document}X167-1 fully splits in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {GF}}\left( 2^{83} \right) $$\end{document}GF283, where we have the decomposition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{167} - 1 = \prod _{\zeta \in \mu _{167}} X - \zeta $$\end{document}X167-1=∏ζ∈μ167X-ζ, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{167}$$\end{document}μ167 is the set of 167-th roots of unity. Hence by the Chinese Remainder Theorem, we obtain the isomorphism\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\text {GF}}\left( 2^{83} \right) [X] / \langle X^{167} - 1 \rangle \rightarrow \bigoplus _{\zeta \in \mu _{167}} {\text {GF}}\left( 2^{83} \right) , \ g \mapsto (g(\zeta ))_{\zeta \in \mu _{167}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}GF283[X]/⟨X167-1⟩→⨁ζ∈μ167GF283,g↦(g(ζ))ζ∈μ167.From this isomorphism, we conclude that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\text {GL}}_3 \left( {\text {GF}}\left( 2^{83} \right) [X] / \langle X^{167} - 1 \rangle \right) \cong \bigoplus _{\zeta \in \mu _{167}} {\text {GL}}_3 \left( {\text {GF}}\left( 2^{83} \right) \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}GL3GF283[X]/⟨X167-1⟩≅⨁ζ∈μ167GL3GF283.By Lagrange, the order of every element in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\text {GL}}_3 \left( {\text {GF}}\left( 2^{83} \right) [X] / \langle X^{167} - 1 \rangle \right) $$\end{document}GL3GF283[X]/⟨X167-1⟩ must divide17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \#{\text {GL}}_3 \left( {\text {GF}}\left( 2^{83} \right) \right) = \left( 2^{3 \cdot 83} - 1 \right) \cdot \left( 2^{3 \cdot 83} - 2^{83} \right) \cdot \left( 2^{3 \cdot 83} - 2^{2 \cdot 83} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}#GL3GF283=23·83-1·23·83-283·23·83-22·83,hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ must be a divisor of (17).

Using Sagemath, we verified that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \mid \left( 2^{3 \cdot 83} - 1 \right) \cdot \left( 2^{3 \cdot 83} - 2^{83} \right) $$\end{document}λ∣23·83-1·23·83-283. Note that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\left( 2^{3 \cdot 83} - 1 \right) \cdot \left( 2^{3 \cdot 83} - 2^{83} \right) \\&= \left( 2^{83} - 1 \right) \cdot \left( 2^{2 \cdot 83} + 2^{83} + 1 \right) \cdot 2^{83} \cdot \left( 2^{2 \cdot 83} - 1 \right) \\&= \left( 2^{83} - 1 \right) \cdot \left( 2^{2 \cdot 83} + 2^{83} + 1 \right) \cdot 2^{83} \cdot (2^{83} + 1) \cdot \left( 2^{83} - 1 \right) \\&= \left( 2^{83} - 1 \right) ^2 \cdot 2^{83} \cdot \left( \left( 2^{2 \cdot 83} + 2^{83} + 1 \right) \cdot \left( 2^{83} + 1 \right) \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}23·83-1·23·83-283=283-1·22·83+283+1·283·22·83-1=283-1·22·83+283+1·283·(283+1)·283-1=283-12·283·22·83+283+1·283+1.Again using Sagemath, we verified that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \mid \left( 2^{2 \cdot 83} + 2^{83} + 1 \right) \cdot \left( 2^{83} + 1 \right) $$\end{document}λ∣22·83+283+1·283+1. By exhaustive search over the divisors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( 2^{2 \cdot 83} + 2^{83} + 1 \right) \cdot \left( 2^{83} + 1 \right) $$\end{document}22·83+283+1·283+1, we managed to find \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ. The details of the code used to compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ can be found in Appendix C.

Discussion: cryptographic implications

The original motivation for this paper was to find a solid mathematical explanation why the order of the linear layer of Xoodoo is relatively low, as this could indicate a potential weakness against invariant subspace attacks [2]. The commutative algebraic reformulation not only provides this explanation, but gave us a deeper insight in the algebraic structure of circulant-like mappings, which is a popular choice for many cryptographic schemes. We believe that we can use our reformulation to find submodules of circulant modules which behave nicely given a module homomorphism. This can mean for example that the submodules stay invariant, or where the images contain a certain structure which remain invariant after multiple propagations. A potentially interesting research topic related to Xoodoo is to find such modules and invariant properties of a given circulant module homomorphism (e.g. the linear layer of Xoodoo), and to which extend they remain invariant when applying the non-linear map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi $$\end{document}χ. This could have interesting cryptographic implications, as we believe such properties can be used to develop potentially effective distinguishers for Xoodoo-like primitives, where its linear layer relies on circulant-like mappings followed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi $$\end{document}χ.

Another potentially interesting application of this module theoretic approach is to see how this can be applied to study the linear components of the hash function Troika [7], where they work over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_3$$\end{document}F3 instead of the binary field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_2$$\end{document}F2. They adapt a variation of column parity mixers over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_3$$\end{document}F3, which we believe can also be reinterpreted using our module-theoretic setting. This could also be an interesting topic of research related to our work.

Concluding remarks

There are two main reasons why the order of the linear layer of Xoodoo is relatively low. These being that the linear layer of Xoodoo is contained in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ker (q^*_{3,R_{4,32}})$$\end{document}ker(q3,R4,32∗), and that the circulant ring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{4,32}$$\end{document}R4,32 is local. Example 2 of the DCD-compositions demonstrated that for a non-local circulant ring, one can construct DCD-compositions with a much higher order than the linear layer of Xoodoo. An interesting follow up research topic would be to study algebraic properties of non-local circulant rings, and to use these properties to experiment in constructing high order DCD-compositions.
